# Michael Jackson

**Michael Joseph Jackson** (August 29, 1958 – June 25, 2009) was an American singer, songwriter, dancer, and philanthropist. Dubbed the "**King of Pop**", he is widely regarded as [one of the most culturally significant figures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_impact_of_Michael_Jackson) of the 20th century. [His musical achievements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Michael_Jackson_records_and_achievements) broke [American racial barriers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_African-American_firsts) and made him [a dominant figure worldwide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Jackson_fandom#Global_fan_presence). Through [his songs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_songs_recorded_by_Michael_Jackson), [music videos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Jackson_music_videos), [concerts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Michael_Jackson_concerts), and fashion, he transformed visual performance in [popular music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_music), popularizing [street dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Street_dance) moves such as the [moonwalk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moonwalk_\(dance\)), the [robot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robot_\(dance\)), and the [anti-gravity lean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-gravity_lean). Jackson is often deemed the greatest entertainer of all time.[^cite_note-7]

The eighth of ten children in the [Jackson family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackson_family), Jackson made his public debut at age six as the lead singer of [the Jackson 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Jackson_5). He rose to solo stardom with *[Off the Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Off_the_Wall)* (1979) and achieved unprecedented global success with *[Thriller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thriller_\(album\))* (1982), which became the [best-selling album](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_best-selling_albums) in history. The music videos for "[Thriller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thriller_\(song\))", "[Beat It](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beat_It)", and "[Billie Jean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billie_Jean)" redefined the medium. *[Bad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bad_\(album\))* (1987) was the first album to produce five US [*Billboard* Hot 100](423161.md) number-one singles: "[I Just Can't Stop Loving You](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I_Just_Can't_Stop_Loving_You)", "[Bad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bad_\(Michael_Jackson_song\))", "[The Way You Make Me Feel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Way_You_Make_Me_Feel)", "[Man in the Mirror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Man_in_the_Mirror)", and "[Dirty Diana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dirty_Diana)". *[Dangerous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dangerous_\(Michael_Jackson_album\))* (1991) and *[HIStory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIStory)* (1995) were also successful. Jackson's final album, [*Invincible*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invincible_\(Michael_Jackson_album\)) (2001), is the [most expensive ever made](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_most_expensive_albums).

From the mid-1980s, Jackson came under public scrutiny for [his relationships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personal_relationships_of_Michael_Jackson), lifestyle, and [changing appearance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_and_appearance_of_Michael_Jackson). He [was accused](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_Michael_Jackson_sexual_abuse_allegations) of sexually abusing a child in 1993 and settled out of court in 1994. In 2005, he [was tried and acquitted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trial_of_Michael_Jackson) on other child sexual abuse charges. While preparing for [This Is It](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/This_Is_It_\(concert_residency\)), a series of comeback concerts, [he died](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_of_Michael_Jackson) in 2009 from an overdose of [propofol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propofol) administered by his personal physician, [Conrad Murray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conrad_Murray). His death caused unprecedented surges in internet traffic and a spike in music sales. Posthumous Jackson releases include the documentary *[Michael Jackson's This Is It](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Jackson's_This_Is_It)* (2009) and the albums *[Michael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_\(Michael_Jackson_album\))* (2010) and *[Xscape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xscape_\(album\))* (2014). The 2019 documentary *[Leaving Neverland](59625891.md)* detailed further child sexual abuse allegations against Jackson.

Jackson is one of the [best-selling music artists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_best-selling_music_artists) of all time, with estimated sales of more than 500 million records worldwide as a solo artist.[^cite_note-10] He is also one of three musicians, alongside [Paul McCartney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_McCartney) and [Phil Collins](153557.md), to have sold more than 100 million records both as a solo artist and as a principal member of a band. He scored [13 number-one singles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artists_with_the_most_number-ones_on_the_U.S._Billboard_Hot_100_chart) on the *Billboard* Hot 100 and is the only artist to have a US top-ten single in six decades. [His accolades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_awards_and_nominations_received_by_Michael_Jackson) include 13 [Grammy Awards](44635.md), the [Grammy Legend Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammy_Legend_Award), the [Grammy Lifetime Achievement Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammy_Lifetime_Achievement_Award), 26 [American Music Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Music_Awards), 12 [World Music Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Music_Awards), 8 [MTV Video Music Awards](295217.md), 6 [Brit Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brit_Awards), and 3 US presidential honors. He was inducted into the [Rock and Roll Hall of Fame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Rock_and_Roll_Hall_of_Fame_inductees) twice, as well as the [Songwriters Hall of Fame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Songwriters_Hall_of_Fame) and the [Dance Hall of Fame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Dance_and_Hall_of_Fame). Having [donated an estimated \$500 million](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philanthropy_of_Michael_Jackson), Jackson is credited with setting a standard for celebrity charity. The 2026 film *[Michael](70014183.md)* is the [highest-grossing biographical film](83066538.md#Highest-grossing_biopics) of all time, while Jackson's [music catalog remains the highest-selling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_music_deals#List_of_largest_music_catalog_acquisitions) for any solo musician.

## Life and career

### Early life and the Jackson 5 (1958–1975)



[Jackson's childhood home](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Jackson_Childhood_Home) in [Gary, Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary,_Indiana), as seen in 2014

Michael Joseph Jackson[^cite_note-11][^cite_note-allmusic-12] was born on August 29, 1958, in [Gary, Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary,_Indiana).[^cite_note-Barnes-13][^cite_note-14] He was the eighth of ten children in the [Jackson family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackson_family), an [African-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American) working-class household living in [a two-bedroom home](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Jackson_Childhood_Home) on Jackson Street.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200926-15][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung200918-16] His mother, [Katherine Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katherine_Jackson) (née Scruse), a [Jehovah's Witness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehovah's_Witness),[^cite_note-monstrous-17] played clarinet, cello, and piano; she aspired to be a [country-and-western](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Country_music) performer, and worked part-time at [Sears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sears).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung200917,_19-18] His father, [Joe Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Jackson_\(talent_manager\)), was a former boxer and a crane operator at [US Steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Steel), who also played guitar for the local [rhythm and blues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhythm_and_blues) group the [Falcons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Falcons).[^cite_note-Obit_Guardian-19][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung200918–19-20] Through his father, Michael had [Native American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American) ([Choctaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Choctaw)) ancestry; his great-grandfather, Israel Nero Jackson, utilized and sold traditional Indigenous herbal medicine, a practice passed down from his father,[^cite_note-21] July "Jack" Gale, an US Army scout who had married a Black slave named Gina before the [Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War).[^cite_note-22] Michael had three sisters—[Rebbie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebbie), [La Toya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Toya_Jackson), and [Janet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janet_Jackson)—and five brothers [Jackie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackie_Jackson), [Tito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tito_Jackson), [Jermaine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jermaine_Jackson), [Marlon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marlon_Jackson), and [Randy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Randy_Jackson_\(Jacksons_singer\)).[^cite_note-Obit_Guardian-19] A sixth brother, Brandon, Marlon's twin, died shortly after birth.[^cite_note-23]

Michael and Marlon joined the Jackson Brothers in 1964, a band their father formed that already included Jackie, Tito, and Jermaine. They initially performed as backup musicians, playing [congas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conga) and [tambourine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tambourine).[^cite_note-24][^cite_note-25] Michael later said that his father physically and emotionally abused him, recalling that during rehearsals Joe often sat with a belt in hand, ready to punish mistakes.[^cite_note-monstrous-17][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELewis_Jones2005165–168-26] Joe acknowledged that he regularly whipped him.[^cite_note-27] Jackie, Tito, Jermaine, and Marlon denied that their father was abusive, saying the whippings—felt more intensely by Michael because he was younger—kept the group disciplined.[^cite_note-28] Michael described his youth as lonely.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung200924-29]

By late 1965, Michael began sharing lead vocals with Jermaine, and the group's name was changed to [the Jackson 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Jackson_5).[^cite_note-30] They won a talent show that year in which Michael danced to [Robert Parker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Parker_\(singer\))'s "[Barefootin'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barefootin'_\(song\))" and sang [the Temptations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Temptations)' "[My Girl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/My_Girl_\(The_Temptations_song\))".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli200913–14-31] From 1966 to 1968, the Jackson 5 toured throughout the [Midwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midwest), performing frequently on the [Chitlin' Circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chitlin'_Circuit) as an opening act for artists such as [Sam & Dave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_&_Dave), [the O'Jays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_O'Jays), [Gladys Knight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gladys_Knight), and [Etta James](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Etta_James). They also performed in clubs and cocktail lounges that featured [striptease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Striptease) shows, as well as at local auditoriums and high school dances.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung200921–22-32][^cite_note-33] In August 1967, while touring the [East Coast](509486.md), they won a weekly amateur night competition at the [Apollo Theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_Theater) in [Harlem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harlem).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung200922-34]



Jackson (center) as a member of [the Jackson 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Jackson_5) in 1972. The group were among the first [African American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_American) performers to attain a large following.

[^cite_note-35]

The Jackson 5 recorded several songs for the Gary-based label [Steeltown Records](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steeltown_Records), releasing their first single, "[Big Boy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Boy_\(song\))", in 1968.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung200921-36] Bobby Taylor of [Bobby Taylor & the Vancouvers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobby_Taylor_&_the_Vancouvers) brought the group to [Motown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motown) after they opened for him at Chicago's [Regal Theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regal_Theater,_Chicago) that year. Taylor produced some of their early Motown recordings, including a version of "[Who's Lovin' You](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Who's_Lovin'_You)".[^cite_note-37] After signing with Motown, the Jackson family relocated to Los Angeles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli200959–69-38] In 1969, Motown executives decided that [Diana Ross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diana_Ross) would introduce the Jackson 5 to the public—partly to support her emerging television career—launching what was considered Motown's final product of its "production line".[^cite_note-39] The group made its first television appearance that year at the [Miss Black America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miss_Black_America) pageant, performing a cover of "[It's Your Thing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/It's_Your_Thing)".[^cite_note-40] *[Rolling Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rolling_Stone)* later described the young Michael as "a prodigy" with "overwhelming musical gifts" who quickly became the group's main attraction and lead singer.[^cite_note-Bio-41]

In January 1970, "[I Want You Back](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I_Want_You_Back)" became the Jackson 5's first single to reach number one on the [*Billboard* Hot 100](423161.md), remaining at the top for four weeks. Three additional Motown singles—"[ABC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABC_\(The_Jackson_5_song\))", "[The Love You Save](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Love_You_Save)", and "[I'll Be There](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I'll_Be_There_\(The_Jackson_5_song\))"—also reached number one.[^cite_note-42] In May 1971, the Jackson family moved into a large home on [Hayvenhurst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hayvenhurst) Avenue, a 2-acre (0.81 ha) estate in [Encino, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encino,_California).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli200981–82-43] During this period, Michael developed from a child performer into a [teen idol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teen_idol).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung200925-44] Between 1972 and 1975, he released four solo studio albums with Motown: *[Got to Be There](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Got_to_Be_There)* (1972), *[Ben](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_\(Michael_Jackson_album\))* (1972), *[Music & Me](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_&_Me)* (1973), and *[Forever, Michael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forever,_Michael)* (1975).[^cite_note-McNulty-45] "[Got to Be There](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Got_to_Be_There_\(song\))" and "[Ben](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_\(song\))", the title tracks from his first two solo albums, sold well as singles, as did his cover of [Bobby Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobby_Day)'s "[Rockin' Robin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockin'_Robin_\(song\))".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli200998–99-46]

Michael remained involved with the Jackson 5.[^cite_note-McNulty-45] The group was later described as "a cutting-edge example of black [crossover artists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crossover_music)".[^cite_note-47] They grew frustrated with Motown's refusal to grant them creative input.[^cite_note-48] Michael's performance of their top five single "[Dancing Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dancing_Machine)" on *[Soul Train](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul_Train)* helped popularize the [robot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robot_\(dance\)) dance.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESt_John200451-49][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMansour2005403-50]

### Move to Epic and *Off the Wall* (1975–1981)



Jackson (left) and [Quincy Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quincy_Jones) (right) appear with [Diana Ross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diana_Ross) on her upcoming special, *Diana*, in March 1981.

The Jackson 5 left Motown in 1975, signing with [Epic Records](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epic_Records) and renaming themselves the Jacksons.[^cite_note-51] Their younger brother Randy joined the band around this time, while Jermaine remained with Motown to pursue a solo career.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli2009138–144-52] The Jacksons continued to tour internationally and released six more albums between 1976 and 1984. Michael, the group's main songwriter during this period, wrote songs such as "[Shake Your Body (Down to the Ground)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shake_Your_Body_\(Down_to_the_Ground\))" (1978), "[This Place Hotel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/This_Place_Hotel)" (1980), and "[Can You Feel It](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Can_You_Feel_It_\(The_Jacksons_song\))" (1980).[^cite_note-53]

In 1977, Jackson moved to [New York City](645042.md) to star as the [Scarecrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scarecrow_\(Oz\)) in *[The Wiz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wiz_\(film\))*, a musical film directed by [Sidney Lumet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sidney_Lumet) and featuring [Diana Ross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diana_Ross), [Nipsey Russell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nipsey_Russell), and Ted Ross.[^cite_note-54] The film was a box-office failure.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli2009178–179-55] Its score was arranged by [Quincy Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quincy_Jones),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBronson2003207-56] who later produced three of Jackson's solo albums.[^cite_note-Who's_Bad-57] During his time in New York, Jackson frequented the [Studio 54](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Studio_54) nightclub, where he heard early [hip-hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hip-hop); this influenced his [beatboxing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beatboxing) on later tracks such as "[Working Day and Night](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Working_Day_and_Night)".[^cite_note-58]

Jackson's fifth solo album and first as an adult, *[Off the Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Off_the_Wall)* (1979), established him as a solo performer and marked his transition from the [bubblegum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bubblegum_music) pop of his youth to more complex sounds.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung200925-44] The album produced four top-10 entries in the US: "[Off the Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Off_the_Wall_\(Michael_Jackson_song\))", "[She's Out of My Life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/She's_Out_of_My_Life)", and the chart-topping hits "[Don't Stop 'Til You Get Enough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don't_Stop_'Til_You_Get_Enough)" and "[Rock with You](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_with_You)".[^cite_note-FourUSTop10s-59] *Off the Wall* reached number three on the [*Billboard* 200](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billboard_200) and sold more than 20 million copies worldwide.[^cite_note-60] Jackson won the Grammy Award for Best Male R\&B Vocal Performance for 1979 with "Don't Stop 'Til You Get Enough".[^cite_note-grammy_mj-61] At the [American Music Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Music_Award) during the early 1980s, he won Favorite Soul/R\&B Single for "Don't Stop 'Til You Get Enough", two consecutive awards for Favorite Soul/R\&B Album, and Favorite Soul/R\&B Male Artist.[^cite_note-62][^cite_note-63] In 1981, he again won the American Music Awards for Favorite Soul/R\&B Album and Favorite Soul/R\&B Male Artist.[^cite_note-64]

Jackson felt that *Off the Wall* should have had a greater influence and was determined to exceed expectations with his next release.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli2009188-65] In 1980, he secured the highest [royalty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royalties) rate in the music industry: 37 percent of wholesale album profit.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli2009191-66]

### *Thriller*, *Motown 25: Yesterday, Today, Forever* and Pepsi incident (1981–1984)

Jackson demonstrating the [moonwalk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moonwalk_\(dance\)) on the 1983 television special *[Motown 25: Yesterday, Today, Forever](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motown_25:_Yesterday,_Today,_Forever)*, which helped popularize the move

From 1981 to 1983, Jackson recorded demos of "[State of Shock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_Shock_\(song\))", "Victory" and "[There Must Be More to Life Than This](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/There_Must_Be_More_to_Life_Than_This)" with [Queen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_\(band\))'s lead singer [Freddie Mercury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freddie_Mercury) for a planned album duet. The project was never completed; "State of Shock" was later recorded with [Mick Jagger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mick_Jagger) for the Jacksons' album *[Victory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victory_\(The_Jacksons_album\))* (1984), and "There Must Be More to Life Than This" was released posthumously in 2014. In 1982, Jackson contributed "Someone in the Dark" to the [audiobook for the film *E.T. the Extra-Terrestrial*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E.T._the_Extra-Terrestrial_\(album\)).[^cite_note-67][^cite_note-68][^cite_note-Greene-69][^cite_note-70]

Jackson's sixth studio album, *[Thriller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thriller_\(album\))*, was released on November 29, 1982. It was the best-selling album worldwide in 1983[^cite_note-71][^cite_note-72] and eventually became the best-selling album of all time in the US[^cite_note-73] and [globally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_best-selling_albums), with an estimated 70 million copies sold.[^cite_note-74] It topped the *Billboard* 200 for 37 weeks and remained in the chart's top 10 for 80 consecutive weeks. *Thriller* became the first album to generate seven top-ten singles on the *Billboard* Hot 100, including the number-one hits "[Billie Jean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billie_Jean)" and "[Beat It](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beat_It)".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELewis_Jones200547-75]

On March 25, 1983, Jackson reunited with his brothers for *[Motown 25: Yesterday, Today, Forever](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motown_25:_Yesterday,_Today,_Forever)*, an NBC television special that aired on May 16 to an estimated audience of 47 million.[^cite_note-76] Jackson's solo performance of "Billie Jean" earned him his first [Emmy Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmy_Award) nomination.[^cite_note-emmys.tv-77] Wearing a [rhinestone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhinestone)-studded glove,[^cite_note-78] he debuted the [moonwalk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moonwalk_\(dance\))—taught to him three years earlier by [Jeffrey Daniel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeffrey_Daniel)—which became his signature dance move.[^cite_note-Daniel-79] Jackson had initially declined the invitation, believing he had been doing too much television, but agreed to perform at the request of Motown founder [Berry Gordy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berry_Gordy) in exchange for a solo spot.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli2009234–237-80] His performance received widespread acclaim: *Rolling Stone*'s Mikal Gilmore called it "extraordinary", and *[The New York Times](30680.md)* critic [Anna Kisselgoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anna_Kisselgoff) praised the timing and technique of his dancing,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung200925-44][^cite_note-81] while Gordy said he was "mesmerized".[^cite_note-82]



Jackson at the [26th Grammy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/26th_Annual_Grammy_Awards), where he won eight awards, breaking the record for the most wins in a single night

At the [26th Annual Grammy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/26th_Annual_Grammy_Awards), Jackson received 12 nominations—the most in a single night at the time (later tied with [Babyface](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babyface_\(musician\)))—and won eight awards, breaking the record for the most wins in a single night (later tied by [Santana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santana_\(band\))).[^cite_note-grammy_mj-61] *Thriller* received 13 nominations, the most for any album, and won eight awards, including Best Engineered Recording (Non-Classical) for [Bruce Swedien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruce_Swedien)'s work.[^cite_note-83] Jackson won seven awards related to the album, including Album of the Year for *Thriller* and Record of the Year for "Beat It", and also won an award for the *E.T. the Extra-Terrestrial* storybook.[^cite_note-grammy_mj-61] At the [11th Annual American Music Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Music_Awards_of_1984), Jackson won another eight awards and became the youngest artist to receive the Award of Merit.[^cite_note-Awards_1984-84] He also won Favorite Male Artist, Favorite Soul/R\&B Artist, and Favorite Pop/Rock Artist. "Beat It" won Favorite Soul/R\&B Video, Favorite Pop/Rock Video, and Favorite Pop/Rock Single. The album won Favorite Soul/R\&B Album and Favorite Pop/Rock Album.[^cite_note-Awards_1984-84][^cite_note-85] *Thriller*'s sales doubled after the release of an extended music video for [its title track](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Jackson's_Thriller_\(music_video\)), which features Jackson dancing with a group of zombies.[^cite_note-86][^cite_note-Griffin-87]

The success transformed Jackson into a dominant force in global pop culture,[^cite_note-Griffin-87] and the album "conquered racial divides".[^cite_note-88] He had the highest royalty rate in the music industry at that point, earning about \$2 for every album sold (equivalent to \$6 in 2025), and was making record-breaking profits.[^cite_note-Time-89] In the same year, *The Making of Michael Jackson's Thriller*, a documentary about the music video, won a Grammy for Best Music Video (Longform).[^cite_note-grammy_mj-61] *Time* described Jackson's influence at the time as "star of records, radio, rock video. A one-man rescue team for the music business. A songwriter who sets the beat for a decade. A dancer with the fanciest feet on the street. A singer who cuts across all boundaries of taste and style and color too."[^cite_note-Time-89] *The New York Times* wrote, "in the world of pop music, there is Michael Jackson and there is everybody else".[^cite_note-90]

Jackson and his brothers partnered with [PepsiCo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PepsiCo) during November 1983 for a \$5 million promotional deal, setting a new record for a celebrity endorsement (equivalent to \$16.2 million in 2025). The first [Pepsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pepsi) campaign, which ran in the US from 1983 to 1984 and launched the company's "New Generation" theme, included tour sponsorship, public relations events, and in-store displays. Jackson helped create the advertisement and suggested using his song "Billie Jean", with revised lyrics, as its [jingle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jingle).[^cite_note-Herrera2-91]



Jackson performing with the Jacksons in [Los Angeles](18110.md) during the [Victory Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victory_Tour_\(The_Jacksons\)) in 1984

On January 27, 1984, Michael and other members of the Jacksons filmed a Pepsi commercial at the [Shrine Auditorium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shrine_Auditorium) in Los Angeles, overseen by [Phil Dusenberry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phil_Dusenberry),[^cite_note-92] a [BBDO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBDO) ad agency executive, and [Alan Pottasch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Pottasch), Pepsi's Worldwide Creative Director. During a simulated concert before a full audience, pyrotechnics accidentally set Jackson's hair on fire, causing second-degree [burns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burn) to his scalp. He underwent treatment to conceal the scars and had his third rhinoplasty shortly afterward. Pepsi settled out of court, and Jackson donated the \$1.5 million settlement (equivalent to \$4.6 million in 2025) to the [Brotman Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brotman_Medical_Center) in [Culver City, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culver_City,_California); its now-closed Michael Jackson Burn Center was named in his honor.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli2009279–287-93][^cite_note-94] Jackson signed a second agreement with Pepsi in the late 1980s for \$10 million (equivalent to \$28.3 million in 2025). The second campaign covered 20 countries and provided financial support for Jackson's *Bad* album and 1987–1988 world tour. Jackson also had endorsements and advertising deals with companies such as [LA Gear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LA_Gear), [Suzuki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suzuki), and [Sony](26989.md), but none were as significant as his deals with Pepsi.[^cite_note-Herrera2-91]

The [Victory Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victory_Tour_\(The_Jacksons\)) of 1984 headlined the Jacksons and showcased Jackson's new solo material to more than two million Americans. It was the last tour he performed with his brothers.[^cite_note-95] Following [controversy over the concert's ticket sales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victory_Tour_\(The_Jacksons_tour\)#Ticket_controversy_and_other_business_issues), Jackson donated his share of the proceeds—estimated at \$3 to 5 million—to charity.[^cite_note-Humanitarian-96] During the final concert of the Victory Tour at the [Dodger Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dodger_Stadium) in Los Angeles, Jackson announced his split from the Jacksons during "Shake Your Body".[^cite_note-97]

### "We Are the World" and other commercial activities (1985)



Jackson signing a "[We Are the World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/We_Are_the_World)" poster in 1985

With [Lionel Richie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lionel_Richie), Jackson co-wrote the charity single "[We Are the World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/We_Are_the_World)" (1985), which raised money for the poor in the US and Africa.[^cite_note-WATW-98][^cite_note-jdoyle-99] It earned \$63 million (equivalent to \$189 million in 2025),[^cite_note-jdoyle-99] and became one of the [best-selling singles of all time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_best-selling_singles), with 20 million copies sold.[^cite_note-100] It won four Grammy Awards in 1985, including Song of the Year for Jackson and Richie.[^cite_note-WATW-98] Jackson, Jones, and promoter Ken Kragen received special awards for their roles in the song's creation.[^cite_note-WATW-98][^cite_note-101][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECampbell1993114-102][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung2009340–344-103]

Jackson collaborated with [Paul McCartney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_McCartney) in the early 1980s and learned that McCartney was making \$40 million a year from owning the rights to other artists' songs.[^cite_note-jdoyle-99] By 1983, Jackson had begun buying publishing rights to others' songs, but he was selective with his acquisitions, bidding on only a few of the many catalogs offered to him. Jackson's early purchases of [music catalogs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_catalog) and song copyrights, such as the [Sly Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sly_Stone) collection, included "Everyday People" (1968), [Len Barry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Len_Barry)'s "[1-2-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1-2-3_\(Len_Barry_song\))" (1965), and [Dion DiMucci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dion_DiMucci)'s "[The Wanderer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wanderer_\(Dion_song\))" (1961) and "[Runaround Sue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Runaround_Sue)" (1961). In 1984, [Robert Holmes à Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Holmes_%C3%A0_Court) announced he was selling the [ATV Music Publishing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sony/ATV_Music_Publishing#Acquisition) catalog, which comprised the publishing rights to nearly 4,000 songs, including most of [the Beatles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Beatles)'s material.[^cite_note-hilburn-104] In 1981, McCartney had been offered the catalog for £20 million (\$40 million) (equivalent to \$142 million in 2025).[^cite_note-jdoyle-99][^cite_note-mcca_atv-105] Jackson submitted a bid of \$46 million (equivalent to \$143 million in 2025) on November 20, 1984.[^cite_note-hilburn-104] When Jackson and McCartney were unable to make a joint purchase, McCartney did not want to be the sole owner of the Beatles's songs and did not pursue an offer on his own.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli2009333–338-106][^cite_note-mcca_atv-105] Jackson's agents were unable to reach a deal, and in May 1985 they left negotiations after spending more than \$1 million and four months on [due diligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Due_diligence).[^cite_note-hilburn-104]

In June 1985, Jackson and Branca learned that [Charles Koppelman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Koppelman)'s and [Martin Bandier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Bandier)'s The Entertainment Company had made a tentative offer to buy ATV Music for \$50 million. In early August, Holmes à Court contacted Jackson and talks resumed. Jackson's increased bid of \$47.5 million (equivalent to \$142 million in 2025) was accepted because he could close the deal more quickly, having already completed due diligence.[^cite_note-hilburn-104] Jackson agreed to visit Holmes à Court in Australia, where he would appear on the [Channel Seven Perth Telethon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_Seven_Perth_Telethon).[^cite_note-107] His purchase of ATV Music was finalized on August 10, 1985.[^cite_note-jdoyle-99][^cite_note-hilburn-104]

### Increased tabloid speculation (1986–1987)

See also: [Health and appearance of Michael Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_and_appearance_of_Michael_Jackson)

Jackson's skin had been medium-brown during his youth, but from the mid-1980s it gradually became paler. The change drew widespread media coverage, including speculation that he had been [bleaching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skin_whitening) his skin.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECampbell199514–16-108][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParameswaran201175–77-109][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeMello2012152-110] His dermatologist, [Arnold Klein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnold_Klein), said he observed in 1983 that Jackson had [vitiligo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vitiligo),[^cite_note-Rosenberg-111] a condition characterized by patches of skin losing their pigment. He also identified [discoid lupus erythematosus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discoid_lupus_erythematosus) in Jackson. Klein diagnosed Jackson with lupus that year[^cite_note-Rosenberg-111] and with vitiligo in 1986.[^cite_note-112] Vitiligo's drastic effects on the body can cause psychological distress. Jackson used fair-colored makeup[^cite_note-113] and possibly skin-bleaching prescription creams[^cite_note-114] to cover the uneven blotches caused by the illness. The creams would depigment the blotches, and with makeup he could appear very pale.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli2009434–436-115] Jackson said he had not purposely bleached his skin and could not control his vitiligo, adding, "When people make up stories that I don't want to be who I am, it hurts me."[^cite_note-116] He became friends with Klein and Klein's assistant, [Debbie Rowe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debbie_Rowe). Rowe later became Jackson's second wife and the mother of his first two children.[^cite_note-117]

Jackson discussed within his 1988 autobiography and a 1993 interview having only two [rhinoplasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhinoplasty) surgeries and a [cleft chin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleft_chin) surgery. He mentioned losing weight in the early 1980s because of his dietary change to achieve a dancer's body.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson2009229–230-118] Witnesses reported him often being dizzy and speculated he was suffering from [anorexia nervosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anorexia_nervosa). Periods of weight loss became a recurring problem later in his life.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli2009312–313-119] After Jackson died, his mother talked about him first turning to [cosmetic procedures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plastic_surgery#Cosmetic_surgery_procedures) to address his vitiligo and avoid looking like a "spotted cow". She said there were more than the two cosmetic surgeries he claimed, and speculated that her son had become addicted to them.[^cite_note-120]



Jackson with his pet chimpanzee [Bubbles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bubbles_\(chimpanzee\)) in 1986

It was reported during 1986 that Jackson slept in a [hyperbaric chamber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperbaric_medicine) to slow aging. He denied the story,[^cite_note-121] though was alleged to have leaked an image of himself sleeping in a glass chamber. Jackson said it was a promotional shot from an upcoming space opera featuring him, *[Captain EO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captain_EO)*, to *[The National Enquirer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_National_Enquirer)*.[^cite_note-122] It was also reported that he took estrogen injections to keep his voice high and facial hair sparse, proposed to [Elizabeth Taylor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_Taylor) and possibly kept a shrine to her, and had cosmetic surgery on his eyes. Jackson's manager [Frank DiLeo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_DiLeo) denied all of these claims except for having a chamber. DiLeo added, "I don't know if he sleeps in it. I'm not for it. But Michael thinks it's something that's probably healthy for him. He's a bit of a health fanatic."[^cite_note-123]

When Jackson took his pet chimpanzee [Bubbles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bubbles_\(chimpanzee\)) on tour in Japan, the media portrayed him as an aspiring [Disney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney) cartoon character who befriended animals.[^cite_note-124] It was also reported that Jackson had offered to buy the bones of [Joseph Merrick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Merrick) (the "Elephant Man").[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli2009355–361-125] In June 1987, the *[Chicago Tribune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Tribune)* reported Jackson's publicist bidding \$1 million for the skeleton from the [London Hospital Medical College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Hospital_Medical_College) on his behalf. The college maintained the skeleton was not for sale. DiLeo said Jackson had an "absorbing interest" in Merrick, "purely based on his awareness of the ethical, medical and historical significance".[^cite_note-126]

Jackson worked with [George Lucas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Lucas) and [Francis Ford Coppola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Ford_Coppola) on a 17-minute, \$30 million [3D film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3D_film), *[Captain EO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captain_EO)*, which ran from 1986 at [Disneyland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disneyland) and [Epcot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epcot), and later at [Tokyo Disneyland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokyo_Disneyland) and [Euro Disneyland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euro_Disneyland).[^cite_note-127] After being removed in the late 1990s, it returned to the theme park for several years after Jackson's death.[^cite_note-128] As a Jehovah's Witness, Jackson would evangelize door-to-door while wearing a disguise.[^cite_note-129] In 1987, *[Ebony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebony_\(magazine\))* reported that Jackson had disassociated himself from the Jehovah's Witnesses.[^cite_note-130] Katherine Jackson said this might have been because some Witnesses strongly opposed the *Thriller* video,[^cite_note-131] which Michael denounced in a Witness publication in 1984.[^cite_note-132] While former members are usually shunned by their families, Jackson's mother remained in contact with him.[^cite_note-133] In 2001, Jackson told an interviewer he was still a Jehovah's Witness.[^cite_note-134]

### *Bad*, autobiography and Neverland (1987–1990)

A man is singing into a microphone under a spotlight. He wears a blue open-neck shirt over a white T-shirt, and dark pants. There are two colorfully-dressed men on either side of him.

Jackson (center) performing a dance sequence to "[The Way You Make Me Feel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Way_You_Make_Me_Feel)" at the [Bad World Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bad_World_Tour) in 1988

Jackson released his highly anticipated seventh studio album, *[Bad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bad_\(album\))*, on August 31, 1987.[^cite_note-Time2-135] *Bad* sold more than 2.25 million copies in its first week in the US, debuting at number one on the *Billboard* 200, where it remained for six weeks.[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137] It became the first album to produce five US number-one singles: "[I Just Can't Stop Loving You](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I_Just_Can't_Stop_Loving_You)", "[Bad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bad_\(Michael_Jackson_song\))", "[The Way You Make Me Feel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Way_You_Make_Me_Feel)", "[Man in the Mirror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Man_in_the_Mirror)", and "[Dirty Diana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dirty_Diana)". "[Smooth Criminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smooth_Criminal)" reached number seven.[^cite_note-FourUSTop10s-59]

*Bad* won the 1988 Grammy for Best Engineered Recording—Non Classical and the 1990 [Grammy Award for Best Music Video](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammy_Award_for_Best_Music_Video), Short Form for "[Leave Me Alone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leave_Me_Alone_\(Michael_Jackson_song\))".[^cite_note-grammy_mj-61][^cite_note-Bruce-138] Jackson received an Award of Achievement at the American Music Awards in 1989 after *Bad* generated five number-one singles, became the first album to top the charts in 25 countries, and was the best-selling album worldwide in 1987 and 1988.[^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140] The [Bad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bad_\(tour\)) tour ran from September 12, 1987, to January 27, 1989.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELewis_Jones200595–96-141] In Japan, it had 14 sellouts and drew 570,000 people, nearly tripling the previous record for a single tour.[^cite_note-142] The 504,000 people who attended seven sold-out shows at [Wembley Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wembley_Stadium_\(1923\)) set a new [Guinness world record](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guinness_world_record).[^cite_note-143] *Bad* cemented Jackson's status as a dominant music force;[^cite_note-144] the album has sold more than 35 million copies worldwide, placing it among the [best-selling albums of all time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Best-selling_albums_of_all_time).[^cite_note-k923-145][^cite_note-146]

In 1988, Jackson released his autobiography, *[Moonwalk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moonwalk_\(book\))*, with input from Stephen Davis and [Jacqueline Kennedy Onassis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacqueline_Kennedy_Onassis).[^cite_note-147] It sold 200,000 copies,[^cite_note-148] and reached the top of [*The New York Times* Best Seller list](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times_Best_Seller_list).[^cite_note-149] Jackson discussed his childhood, the Jackson 5, and the abuse he experienced from his father.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson200929–31-150] He attributed his changing facial appearance to three plastic surgeries, puberty, weight loss, a strict vegetarian diet, a change in hairstyle, and stage lighting.[^cite_note-151][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson2009229–230-118] On June 28, 1988, during the Bad World Tour, Jackson was presented with the Grand Vermeil [Medal of the City of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medal_of_the_City_of_Paris) by Mayor [Jacques Chirac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Chirac).[^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153] On July 20, he became the first [commoner](24710172.md) in history to enter London's [Guildhall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guildhall,_London) through the building's Royal Entrance.[^cite_note-154] In October, Jackson released the film *[Moonwalker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moonwalker)*, comprising live footage, music videos and narrative sequences Jackson and [Joe Pesci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Pesci). In the US, it was released [direct-to-video](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct-to-video) and became the best-selling music videocassette in history.[^cite_note-155][^cite_note-156] It was certified eight times platinum.[^cite_note-157]



Jackson performing in [Vienna](55866.md) in July 1988

In March 1988, Jackson purchased 2,700 acres (11 km<sup>2</sup>) of land near [Santa Ynez, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Ynez,_California), to build a home, [Neverland Ranch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neverland_Ranch), at a cost of \$17 million (equivalent to \$46 million in 2025).[^cite_note-Malta-158] He installed a [Ferris wheel](175541.md), a [carousel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carousel), a movie theater, and a zoo.[^cite_note-Malta-158][^cite_note-Bio2-159][^cite_note-160] A security staff of 40 patrolled the grounds.[^cite_note-Bio2-159] Shortly afterward, Jackson appeared in the first Western television advertisement broadcast in the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union).[^cite_note-161] Around this time, Jackson became known as the "[King of Pop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honorific_nicknames_in_popular_music)", a nickname embraced by his publicists.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELewis_Jones2005165–168-26][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETannenbaumMarks2011Chapter_41,_"I_Want_to_Have_a_Nickname"-162][^cite_note-ew1991-163] When [Elizabeth Taylor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_Taylor) presented him with the Soul Train Heritage Award in 1989, she called him "the true king of pop, rock and soul".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECampbell1993260–263-164] President [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush) designated him the White House's "Artist of the Decade".[^cite_note-165] At the 38th [BMI Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BMI_Awards) in 1990, Jackson became the first person to be honored with an award named after its recipient.[^cite_note-166] From 1985 to 1990, Jackson donated \$455,000 to the [United Negro College Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Negro_College_Fund),[^cite_note-167] and all profits from his single "Man in the Mirror" went to charity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli2009382-168] His performance of "You Were There" at [Sammy Davis Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sammy_Davis_Jr.)'s 60th birthday celebration earned Jackson a second Emmy nomination.[^cite_note-emmys.tv-77] Jackson was the best-selling artist of the 1980s.[^cite_note-Brooks-169]

### *Dangerous* and public social work (1991–1993)

&#x20;In March 1991, Jackson renewed his contract with Sony for \$65 million (equivalent to \$154 million in 2025), a [record-breaking deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_music_deals) at the time.[^cite_note-170] He released his eighth studio album, *[Dangerous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dangerous_\(Michael_Jackson_album\))*, on November 21, 1991, co-produced with [Teddy Riley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teddy_Riley).[^cite_note-171] It was certified eight times platinum in the US and had sold 32 million copies worldwide by 2018.[^cite_note-Certifications-172][^cite_note-Telegraph2009-173] In the US, the first single, "[Black or White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_or_White)", was the album's highest-charting song; it was number one on the *Billboard* Hot 100 for seven weeks and achieved similar chart success worldwide.[^cite_note-Achievements-174] The second single, "[Remember the Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remember_the_Time)" peaked at number three on the *Billboard* Hot 100.[^cite_note-billboard_mj-175] At the end of 1992, *Dangerous* was the best-selling album of the year worldwide, and "Black or White" the best-selling single of the year worldwide at the [*Billboard* Music Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billboard_Music_Award).[^cite_note-Brooks-169] In 1993, he performed "Remember the Time" at the [Soul Train Music Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul_Train_Music_Awards) while seated, saying he had twisted his ankle during dance rehearsals.[^cite_note-176] In the UK, "[Heal the World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heal_the_World)" reached number two on the charts in 1992.[^cite_note-OC-177]



Jackson during the [Dangerous World Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dangerous_World_Tour) in [Monza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monza), Italy, in 1992

Jackson founded the [Heal the World Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heal_the_World_Foundation) in 1992. The charity brought underprivileged children to Jackson's ranch to use the theme park rides and sent millions of dollars around the world to help children threatened by war, poverty, and disease. That July, Jackson published his second book, *[Dancing the Dream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dancing_the_Dream)*, a collection of poetry. In support of *Dangerous*, Jackson embarked on his second world tour, the [Dangerous World Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dangerous_World_Tour), to raise money for his Heal the World Foundation.[^cite_note-Zad-1992-178] The tour ran from June 1992 to November 1993, grossing \$100 million (equivalent to \$220 million in 2025) from 69 concerts outside the US and attracting more than 3.5 million people.[^cite_note-179][^cite_note-180] Jackson sold the broadcast rights for his October 1, 1992, concert in Bucharest to [HBO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HBO) for \$20 million (equivalent to \$46 million in 2025), then the highest fee ever paid for a concert performer to appear on television.[^cite_note-181] He donated a portion of the fee to aid Romanian children.[^cite_note-Zad-1992-178]

Following the death of [HIV/AIDS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIV/AIDS) spokesperson and friend [Ryan White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ryan_White), Jackson urged the Clinton administration at [Bill Clinton's inaugural gala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_inauguration_of_Bill_Clinton) to increase funding for HIV/AIDS charities and research,[^cite_note-182][^cite_note-183] and performed "[Gone Too Soon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gone_Too_Soon)", a song dedicated to White, and "Heal the World" at the gala.[^cite_note-184] Jackson visited Africa in early 1992; on his first stop in Gabon, he was greeted by more than 100,000 people, some carrying signs that read "Welcome Home Michael",[^cite_note-Ebony-185] and was awarded the rank of Officer of the [National Order of Merit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Order_of_Merit_\(Gabon\)) from President [Omar Bongo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omar_Bongo).[^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187] During his trip to Ivory Coast, Jackson drew larger crowds than [Pope John Paul II](23805.md) had on his previous visits.[^cite_note-188] He was crowned "King Sani" by a tribal chief in the Ivorian village of [Krindjabo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krindjabo), where he thanked dignitaries in French and English, signed documents formalizing his kingship, and sat on a golden throne while presiding over ceremonial dances.[^cite_note-Ebony-185]

In January 1993, Jackson performed at the [Super Bowl XXVII halftime show](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Bowl_XXVII_halftime_show) in Pasadena, California. The [National Football League](21211.md) (NFL) sought a major artist to maintain viewership during halftime following declining audience figures.[^cite_note-189][^cite_note-190] With 133.4 million viewers, it was the first Super Bowl whose halftime show drew a larger audience than the game itself.[^cite_note-191] Jackson performed "[Jam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jam_\(Michael_Jackson_song\))", "Billie Jean", "Black or White", and "Heal the World". *Dangerous* rose 90 places on the US albums chart following the performance.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECampbell199514–16-108]

Jackson gave [a 90-minute interview](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Jackson_Talks_..._to_Oprah) with [Oprah Winfrey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oprah_Winfrey) on February 10, 1993. He spoke about the abuse he experienced during his childhood at the hands of his father, said he believed he had missed out on much of his childhood, and stated that he often cried from loneliness. He denied tabloid rumors that he had bought the bones of the Elephant Man, slept in a hyperbaric oxygen chamber, or bleached his skin, and stated for the first time that he had vitiligo. After the interview, *Dangerous* re-entered the US albums chart in the top 10, more than a year after its release.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELewis_Jones2005165–168-26][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECampbell199514–16-108] The interview became the [most-watched television interview in US history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_most-watched_television_broadcasts#Most-watched_television_interviews) to date, with more than 90 million viewers.[^cite_note-192]

In January 1993, Jackson won three [American Music Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Music_Awards): Favorite Pop/Rock Album (*Dangerous*), Favorite Soul/R\&B Single ("Remember the Time"), and became the first recipient of the International Artist Award of Excellence.[^cite_note-193][^cite_note-194] In February, he received the "Living Legend Award" at the [35th Annual Grammy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/35th_Annual_Grammy_Awards) in Los Angeles.[^cite_note-grammy_mj-61] He attended the ceremony with [Brooke Shields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brooke_Shields).[^cite_note-195] *Dangerous* was nominated for Best Vocal Performance (for "Black or White"), Best R\&B Vocal Performance ("Jam"), and Best R\&B Song ("Jam"), and Bruce Swedien and Teddy Riley won the Grammy Award for Best Engineered—Non Classical.[^cite_note-Bruce-138] In the decades since, *Dangerous* has been lauded as arguably Jackson's greatest artistic achievement.[^cite_note-196][^cite_note-197][^cite_note-198][^cite_note-199]

### First child sexual abuse accusations and first marriage (1993–1995)

Main article: [1993 Michael Jackson sexual abuse allegations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_Michael_Jackson_sexual_abuse_allegations)

In the second half of 1993, Jackson faced controversy following allegations that he had sexually abused a 13‑year‑old boy, Jordan Chandler, whom he met that year.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli2009496–498-200] On July 8, Jordan's father, Evan Chandler, was recorded threatening to ruin Jackson's career with a "carefully planned plot".[^cite_note-Philips_&_Ferrell-201][^cite_note-202] The following day, Jackson's investigator, [Anthony Pellicano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Pellicano), met Jordan, who denied any abuse or inappropriate touching by Jackson.[^cite_note-203] After a letter from psychiatrist Mathis Abrams stating that sexual abuse might have occurred,[^cite_note-Sullivan_2012-204] Chandler allegedly received a confession from his son[^cite_note-Sullivan_2012-204] and demanded \$20 million from Jackson on August 4, 1993, which Jackson declined.[^cite_note-Philips_&_Ferrell-201] From August 9–13, 1993, Evan Chandler and his legal team made various counteroffers to secure a settlement from Jackson, escalating their financial demands after his refusal to pay the initial \$20 million. Evan later filed a lawsuit in September seeking \$30 million for alleged damages.[^cite_note-205] Jackson denied all of the claims and financial demands, prompting Chandler to go public with the allegations.[^cite_note-206][^cite_note-207][^cite_note-208]

Prosecutors raided Neverland Ranch and other residences while Jackson was on tour in August,[^cite_note-209] finding no [child pornography](25211885.md) or other incriminating evidence.[^cite_note-210][^cite_note-211] In December 1993, Prosecutor [Thomas W. Sneddon Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_W._Sneddon_Jr.) filed a court order to conduct a strip search of Jackson, based on a drawing provided by Jordan and submitted to authorities. According to [Reuters](18998750.md) and *[USA Today](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USA_Today)*, the description of Jackson's genitalia did not match the photographs taken during the police investigation.[^cite_note-212][^cite_note-213][^cite_note-214] The grand jury subsequently subpoenaed Jackson's mother, reportedly to assess whether there were any physical alterations compared to the description.[^cite_note-215][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalperin200984-216][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEbert2010201-217] In November 1993, Jackson requested that the civil trial be postponed until after the criminal hearing, arguing that proceeding would violate his [civil rights](37071.md) under the circumstances of having to defend himself under [double jeopardy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double_jeopardy). The motion was denied, and in January 1994, the civil lawsuit was settled for \$15,331,250, with separate payments of \$1.5 million for Evan and June Chandler, totaling \$18.3 million allocated to the Chandler family—less than the initial \$20–30 million demand.[^cite_note-218][^cite_note-suit-219]

The settlement documents show that the Chandlers dropped the child molestation allegations from their complaint, with Jackson's settlement filed over claims of negligence. It was later revealed that the settlement did not prevent the Chandlers from testifying in the criminal case.[^cite_note-220] The police never filed criminal charges.[^cite_note-221] The prosecution continued to investigate Jackson from January to June 1994, with Jordan continuing to cooperate with investigators.[^cite_note-suit-219] By July 1994, multiple grand juries had been convened; jurors reported that no damaging evidence was presented.[^cite_note-222] On September 21, 1994, after 400 witnesses had been investigated and another 30 called before the grand juries, the investigation was closed. A few potential leads were found, but all proved false.[^cite_note-223]

Jackson had been taking [painkillers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Painkiller) for reconstructive scalp surgeries administered after the 1984 Pepsi commercial accident, and became dependent on them to cope with the stress of the allegations.[^cite_note-224] On November 12, 1993, Jackson canceled the remainder of the Dangerous World Tour due to health problems, stress from the allegations, and painkiller addiction. He thanked his friend Elizabeth Taylor for her support. The end of the tour concluded his sponsorship deal with Pepsi.[^cite_note-225]

In late 1993, Jackson proposed to [Lisa Marie Presley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisa_Marie_Presley), the daughter of [Elvis Presley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elvis_Presley), over the phone.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli2009518–520-226] They were married in [La Vega, Dominican Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Vega,_Dominican_Republic), in May 1994 by civil judge Hugo Francisco Álvarez Pérez.[^cite_note-Wed-227] The tabloid media speculated that the wedding was a publicity stunt intended to deflect attention from Jackson's sexual abuse allegations and to boost Presley's career as a singer.[^cite_note-Wed-227][^cite_note-Divorce-228] Their marriage ended little more than a year later, and they separated in December 1995.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli2009580–581-229] Presley cited "irreconcilable differences" when filing for divorce the following month and sought only to reclaim her maiden name.[^cite_note-Divorce-228][^cite_note-230] She later said they had attempted to reconcile intermittently for four years after their divorce, traveling internationally to be together.[^cite_note-231]

Jackson composed music for the [Sega Genesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sega_Genesis) video game *[Sonic the Hedgehog 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sonic_the_Hedgehog_3)* (1994), but left the project around the time the allegations surfaced and went uncredited. His involvement was unconfirmed until 2022.[^cite_note-232][^cite_note-233] Sega employees said his involvement was terminated following the allegations.[^cite_note-234][^cite_note-235] However, members of Jackson's team said he was unhappy with how the Genesis hardware replicated his music.[^cite_note-236] The *Sonic 3* credits theme was reworked as Jackson's 1996 song "[Stranger in Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stranger_in_Moscow)".[^cite_note-237]

### *HIStory*, second marriage, fatherhood and *Blood on the Dance Floor: HIStory in the Mix* (1995–1997)

Close-up of a pale skinned Jackson with black hair. He is wearing a black jacket with white designs on it.

Jackson at the [1997 Cannes Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_Cannes_Film_Festival) for the premiere of *[Michael Jackson's Ghosts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Jackson's_Ghosts)*

In June 1995, Jackson released the double album *[HIStory: Past, Present and Future, Book I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIStory:_Past,_Present_and_Future,_Book_I)*. The first disc, *HIStory Begins*, is a [greatest hits compilation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greatest_hits_album) (reissued in 2001 as *Greatest Hits: HIStory, Volume I*). The second disc, *HIStory Continues*, contains 13 original songs and two cover versions. The album debuted at number one on the charts and has been certified for eight million shipments in the US.[^cite_note-238] It is one of the best-selling albums of all time, with 20 million copies (40 million units) sold worldwide.[^cite_note-Achievements-174][^cite_note-Telegraph2009-173] *HIStory* received a Grammy nomination for [Album of the Year](196513.md).[^cite_note-grammy_mj-61] *The New York Times* described it as "the testimony of a musician whose self-pity now equals his talent".[^cite_note-239]

The first single from *HIStory* was "[Scream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scream_\(Michael_Jackson_and_Janet_Jackson_song\))/[Childhood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Childhood_\(Michael_Jackson_song\))". "Scream", a duet with Jackson's youngest sister Janet, protests the media's treatment of Jackson during the 1993 child abuse allegations. The single reached number five on the *Billboard* Hot 100[^cite_note-billboard_mj-175] and received a Grammy nomination for Best Pop Collaboration with Vocals.[^cite_note-grammy_mj-61] The second single, "[You Are Not Alone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/You_Are_Not_Alone)", holds the Guinness world record as the first song to debut at number one on the *Billboard* Hot 100.[^cite_note-World_Records-240] It received a Grammy nomination for Best Pop Vocal Performance in 1995.[^cite_note-grammy_mj-61]

In 1995, the [Anti-Defamation League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Defamation_League) and other groups criticized the original lyrics of "[They Don't Care About Us](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/They_Don't_Care_About_Us)" ("Jew me, sue me, everybody do me / Kick me, [kike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kike) me, don't you black or white me") as [antisemitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitism_in_the_United_States). Jackson released a revised version of the song.[^cite_note-241] In late 1995, he was hospitalized after collapsing during rehearsals for a televised performance, the result of a stress‑related [panic attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panic_attack).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli2009576–577-242] In November, Jackson merged his ATV Music catalog with Sony's music publishing division, creating [Sony/ATV Music Publishing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sony/ATV_Music_Publishing). He retained ownership of half the company, earning \$95 million up front (equivalent to \$201 million in 2025) as well as [rights to additional songs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Sony/ATV_Music_Publishing_artists).[^cite_note-243][^cite_note-sonydeal-244]

"[Earth Song](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth_Song)" was the third single released from *HIStory*, and topped the [UK Singles Chart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UK_Singles_Chart) for six weeks over Christmas 1995.[^cite_note-OC-177] It became the 87th-best-selling single in UK history.[^cite_note-245] At the [1996 Brit Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_Brit_Awards), Jackson's performance of "Earth Song" was interrupted by [Pulp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulp_\(band\)) singer [Jarvis Cocker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jarvis_Cocker), who said he was protesting what he viewed as Jackson's "Christ-like" persona. Jackson called the stage invasion "disgusting and cowardly".[^cite_note-246][^cite_note-247]

In 1996, Jackson won a Grammy for Best Music Video, Short Form, for "Scream" and an American Music Award for Favorite Pop/Rock Male Artist.[^cite_note-grammy_mj-61][^cite_note-248] In July 1996, he performed for [Sultan Hassanal Bolkiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultan_Hassanal_Bolkiah)'s 50th birthday at [Jerudong Park Amphitheater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerudong_Park_Amphitheater) in Brunei, which had been built specifically for the event.[^cite_note-249] Jackson was reportedly paid \$17 million (equivalent to \$35 million in 2025).[^cite_note-250] He promoted *HIStory* with the [HIStory World Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIStory_World_Tour), from September 7, 1996, to October 15, 1997. He performed 82 concerts across five continents, 35 countries, and 58 cities to more than 4.5 million fans, making it his most‑attended tour. It grossed \$165 million (equivalent to \$331 million in 2025).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELewis_Jones200595–96-141] During the tour, in Sydney, Australia, Jackson married [Debbie Rowe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debbie_Rowe), a dermatology assistant who was six months pregnant with his first child.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli2009570–586-251]

[Michael Joseph Jackson Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Joseph_Jackson_Jr.) (commonly known as Prince) was born on February 13, 1997. His sister [Paris-Michael Katherine Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris-Michael_Katherine_Jackson) was born on April 3, 1998.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli2009580–581,_597-252] Jackson and Rowe divorced in 2000; Rowe conceded custody of the children and received an \$8.5 million settlement (equivalent to \$15.9 million in 2025).[^cite_note-253] In 2004, after the [second child abuse allegations against Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trial_of_Michael_Jackson), she returned to court seeking to regain custody. The suit was settled in 2006.[^cite_note-Ex_wife-254]

In 1997, Jackson released *[Blood on the Dance Floor: HIStory in the Mix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_on_the_Dance_Floor:_HIStory_in_the_Mix)*, which contained remixes of singles from *HIStory* and five new songs. Worldwide sales stand at 6 million copies, making it the [best-selling remix album](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_best-selling_remix_albums) of all time. It reached number one in the UK, as did the single "[Blood on the Dance Floor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_on_the_Dance_Floor_\(song\))".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERojek200774-255] In the US, the album reached number 24 and was certified platinum.[^cite_note-Certifications-172]

### Label dispute and *Invincible* (1997–2002)

From October 1997 to September 2001, Jackson worked on his tenth solo album, *[Invincible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invincible_\(Michael_Jackson_album\))*, which cost \$30 million (equivalent to \$54.5 million in 2025) to record, making it the [most expensive album of all time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_most_expensive_albums).[^cite_note-Burkeman-256] In June 1999, Jackson joined [Luciano Pavarotti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luciano_Pavarotti) for a [War Child](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_Child_\(charity\)) benefit concert in [Modena](62418.md), Italy. The show raised a million dollars for refugees of the [Kosovo War](16760.md), and additional funds for the children of [Guatemala](17238567.md).[^cite_note-257] Later that month, Jackson organized a series of "[Michael Jackson & Friends](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Jackson_&_Friends)" benefit concerts in Germany and [South Korea](27019.md). Other artists involved included [Slash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slash_\(musician\)), [The Scorpions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scorpions_\(band\)), [Boyz II Men](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boyz_II_Men), [Luther Vandross](249167.md), [Mariah Carey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariah_Carey), [A. R. Rahman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A._R._Rahman), [Prabhu Deva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prabhu_Deva) Sundaram, [Shobana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shobana), [Andrea Bocelli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrea_Bocelli) and Luciano Pavarotti. The proceeds went to the [Nelson Mandela Children's Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nelson_Mandela_Children's_Fund), the [Red Cross](15487.md) and [UNESCO](21786641.md).[^cite_note-258] Jackson was presented with the "Outstanding Humanitarian Award" at the 1999 [Bollywood Movie Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bollywood_Movie_Awards) in New York City where he noted [Mahatma Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahatma_Gandhi) was an inspiration for him.[^cite_note-259][^cite_note-260] At the turn of the century, Jackson was awarded with the Artist of the 1980s Decade Award at the [27th American Music Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/27th_American_Music_Awards),[^cite_note-MTV-261] and the Best Selling Pop Male Artist of the Millennium Award at the 12th [World Music Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Music_Awards).[^cite_note-CIIC-262] In 2000, *[Guinness World Records](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guinness_World_Records)* recognized him for supporting 39 charities, more than any other entertainer.[^cite_note-263]

[Two concerts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Jackson:_30th_Anniversary_Special) were held in September 2001 at Madison Square Garden to mark Jackson's 30th year as a solo artist. Jackson performed with his brothers for the first time since 1984. The show also featured [Mýa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%BDa), [Usher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Usher_\(singer\)), [Whitney Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitney_Houston), [Destiny's Child](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Destiny's_Child), [Monica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monica_\(singer\)), [Liza Minnelli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liza_Minnelli) and Slash. The first show was marred by technical lapses, and the crowd booed a speech by [Marlon Brando](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marlon_Brando).[^cite_note-264] 45 million people watched the television broadcast of the shows in November according to [Nielsen Media Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nielsen_Media_Research).[^cite_note-265] After the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md), which Jackson narrowly avoided death by oversleeping and missing a scheduled meeting at the [World Trade Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Center_\(1973%E2%80%932001\)),[^cite_note-266] he helped organize the [United We Stand: What More Can I Give](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_We_Stand:_What_More_Can_I_Give) benefit concert at [Robert F. Kennedy Memorial Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_F._Kennedy_Memorial_Stadium) in Washington, D.C., on October 21, 2001. Jackson performed "[What More Can I Give](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/What_More_Can_I_Give)" as the finale.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli2009614–617-267]

The release of *Invincible* was preceded by a dispute between Jackson and his record label, Sony Music Entertainment. He had expected the licenses to the masters of his albums to revert to him in the early 2000s, and enable him to keep the profits and promote his material as desired, but clauses in the contract set the revert date years into the future. Jackson sought an early exit from his contract.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli2009610–611-268] *Invincible* was released on October 30, 2001. It was Jackson's first full-length album in six years, and the last album of original material he released in his lifetime.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli2009610–611-268] It debuted at number one in 13 countries, and went on to sell eight million copies worldwide, receiving double-platinum certification in the US.[^cite_note-Certifications-172][^cite_note-269][^cite_note-270]

On January 9, 2002, Jackson won his 22nd American Music Award for Artist of the Century.[^cite_note-271][^cite_note-272] Later that year, an anonymous [surrogate mother](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surrogate_mother) gave birth to his third child, Prince Michael Jackson II (nicknamed "Blanket"), who had been conceived by [artificial insemination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artificial_insemination).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli2009599–600-273] On November 20, Jackson briefly held Blanket over the railing of his Berlin hotel room, four stories above ground level, prompting widespread criticism in the media. Jackson apologized for the incident, calling it "a terrible mistake".[^cite_note-Vineyard-274] On January 22, promoter Marcel Avram filed a breach of contract complaint against Jackson for failing to perform two planned 1999 concerts.[^cite_note-275] In March, a Santa Maria jury ordered Jackson to pay Avram \$5.3 million (equivalent to \$9.5 million in 2025).[^cite_note-276][^cite_note-277] On December 18, 2003, Jackson's attorneys dropped all appeals on the verdict and settled the lawsuit for an undisclosed amount.[^cite_note-278]

On April 24, 2002, Jackson performed at [Apollo Theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_Theater). The concert was a fundraiser for the Democratic National Committee and former President Bill Clinton.[^cite_note-279] The money collected would be used to encourage citizens to vote. It raised \$2.5 million (equivalent to \$3.9 million in 2025).[^cite_note-280] The concert was called [Michael Jackson: Live at the Apollo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Jackson:_Live_at_the_Apollo_2002) and was one of Jackson's final on-stage performances.[^cite_note-281]

In July 2002, at [Al Sharpton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Sharpton)'s [National Action Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Action_Network) in Harlem, Jackson called the Sony Music chairman [Tommy Mottola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tommy_Mottola) "a racist, and very, very, very devilish", and accused him of exploiting black artists for his own gain. The accusation prompted Sharpton to form a coalition investigating whether Mottola exploited black artists.[^cite_note-Mottola-282] Jackson charged that Mottola had called his colleague [Irv Gotti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irv_Gotti) a "fat [nigger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigger)".[^cite_note-283] Sony issued a statement calling the accusations "ludicrous, spiteful, and hurtful" and said Mottola had championed Jackson's career for years.[^cite_note-Mottola-282] Sony refused to renew Jackson's contract and said that a \$25 million (equivalent to \$44.8 million in 2025) promotional campaign had failed because Jackson refused to tour in the US for *Invincible*.[^cite_note-Burkeman-256]

### Documentary, *Number Ones*, second child abuse allegations and acquittal (2002–2005)

Further information: [Trial of Michael Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trial_of_Michael_Jackson)



Jackson in Las Vegas, 2003

Beginning in May 2002, a documentary film crew led by [Martin Bashir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Bashir) followed Jackson for several months.[^cite_note-Vineyard-274] The documentary, broadcast in February 2003 as *[Living with Michael Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Living_with_Michael_Jackson)*, showed Jackson holding hands and discussing sleeping arrangements with a twelve-year-old boy.[^cite_note-284][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaraborrelli2009640-285] He discussed seeing nothing wrong with having sleepovers with minors and sharing his bed and bedroom with various people, which aroused controversy. He insisted that the sleepovers were not sexual and that his words had been misunderstood.[^cite_note-286][^cite_note-287]

In October 2003, Jackson received the [Key to the City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Key_to_the_City) of [Las Vegas](47737.md) from Mayor [Oscar Goodman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oscar_Goodman).[^cite_note-288] On November 18, 2003, Sony released *[Number Ones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Number_Ones_\(Michael_Jackson_album\))*, a [greatest hits album](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greatest_hits_album). It was certified five times platinum in the US and ten times platinum in the UK, for shipments of at least 3 million units.[^cite_note-Certifications-172][^cite_note-289]

On December 18, 2003, Santa Barbara authorities charged Jackson with seven counts of child molestation and two counts of intoxicating a minor with [alcoholic drinks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcoholic_drink).[^cite_note-290] Jackson denied the allegations and pleaded not guilty.[^cite_note-291] The *People v. Jackson* trial began on January 31, 2005, in [Santa Maria, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Maria,_California), and lasted until the end of May. Jackson found the experience stressful and it affected his health. If convicted, he would have faced up to twenty years in prison.[^cite_note-292] On June 13, 2005, Jackson was acquitted on all counts.[^cite_note-293] [FBI files on Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FBI_files_on_Michael_Jackson), released in 2009, showed that the FBI found no evidence of criminal conduct.[^cite_note-294]

### Final years, financial problems, *Thriller 25* and This Is It (2005–2009)

Jackson is wearing an overcoat and walking from left to right. His face is obscured by his hair. His son is wearing a mask and a baseball cap. Two men are with them; a third person is holding an umbrella over the Jacksons.

Jackson and his son Blanket in [Disneyland Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disneyland_Paris), 2006

After the trial, Jackson became reclusive.[^cite_note-295] He moved to [Bahrain](18933277.md) during June 2005 as a guest of [Sheikh Abdullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_bin_Hamad_bin_Isa_Al_Khalifa).[^cite_note-Bishara-2021-296] He never returned to Neverland.[^cite_note-297] In early 2006, it was announced that Jackson had signed a contract with a Bahrain startup, Two Seas Records. Nothing came of the deal, and the Two Seas CEO, [Guy Holmes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gut_Records), later said it was never finalized.[^cite_note-298][^cite_note-Bahrain-299] Holmes also found that Jackson was on the verge of bankruptcy and was involved in 47 ongoing lawsuits.[^cite_note-Bishara-2021-296] By September 2006, Jackson was no longer affiliated with Two Seas.[^cite_note-Bahrain-299]

In April 2006, Jackson agreed to use a piece of his ATV catalog stake, then worth about \$1 billion, as [collateral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collateral_\(finance\)) against his \$270 million worth of loans from [Bank of America](347756.md). Bank of America had sold the loans to [Fortress Investments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortress_Investment_Group), an investment company that buys distressed loans, the year before. As part of the agreement, Fortress Investments provided Jackson a new loan of \$300 million with reduced interest payments (equivalent to \$480 million in 2025). Sony Music would have the option to buy half of his stake, or about 25% of the catalog, at a set price. Jackson's financial managers had urged him to shed part of his stake to avoid bankruptcy.[^cite_note-sonydeal-244][^cite_note-300] The main house at Neverland Ranch was closed as a cost-cutting measure, while Jackson lived in Bahrain in 2005.[^cite_note-301] At least thirty of Jackson's employees had not been paid on time and were owed \$306,000 in back wages.
